# S5.1 P2 Paper 03 — TravelPlanner: A Benchmark for Real-World Planning with Language Agents

## Metadata

- **Title:** TravelPlanner: A Benchmark for Real-World Planning with Language Agents
- **Year:** 2024
- **Verified venue/status:** ICML 2024 Spotlight
- **Peer-reviewed status:** Yes
- **Corrected action:** Upgrade to peer-reviewed
- **Thesis section:** S5.1 — Evaluation and Benchmarks for LLM-based Agents
- **Main category:** real-world planning benchmark
- **S5.1 role:** agent evaluation methodology source
- **Priority:** P2
- **Recommended citation strength:** Strong citation
- **Recommended reading depth:** Medium to high
- **BibTeX key:** `travelplannerrealworldplanning2024`

---

## Simple understanding

TravelPlanner evaluates whether language agents can produce realistic travel plans under constraints such as time, location, budget, and user preferences. It is important because it exposes the weakness of LLM agents on long-horizon planning in a real-world-like domain.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Formulate travel planning as a realistic benchmark where agents must satisfy multiple constraints and generate coherent itineraries.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
real-world planning benchmark
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

LLM agents can produce plausible plans, but they often violate constraints, miss dependencies, or fail to maintain consistency across long-horizon planning steps.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

- more realistic environments,
- longer task horizons,
- multimodal perception,

- evaluated models or scaffolds,

- failure taxonomy,
- and authors' stated limitations.

- **Limitation 1:** Travel planning is one domain; results may not generalize to all web or enterprise tasks.
- **Limitation 2:** Plans can look fluent while still violating hidden constraints.
- **Limitation 3:** The benchmark evaluates planning output more than real browser execution.

# S5.1 P2 Paper 04 — AndroidWorld: A Dynamic Benchmarking Environment for Autonomous Agents

## Metadata

- **Title:** AndroidWorld: A Dynamic Benchmarking Environment for Autonomous Agents
- **Year:** 2024/2025
- **Verified venue/status:** ICLR 2025
- **Peer-reviewed status:** Yes
- **Corrected action:** Upgrade to peer-reviewed
- **Thesis section:** S5.1 — Evaluation and Benchmarks for LLM-based Agents
- **Main category:** dynamic Android agent benchmark
- **S5.1 role:** GUI/mobile-agent benchmark
- **Priority:** P2
- **Recommended citation strength:** Strong citation
- **Recommended reading depth:** Medium to high
- **BibTeX key:** `androidworlddynamicenvironmentautonomous2024`

---

## Simple understanding

AndroidWorld is a dynamic Android benchmark for autonomous agents. It evaluates whether agents can interact with real or realistic Android apps through observations and actions.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Provide an executable Android environment where agents complete mobile tasks under dynamic UI conditions.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
dynamic Android agent benchmark
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Even strong agents struggle with mobile UI grounding, state tracking, and multi-step execution in dynamic Android environments.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

- more realistic environments,
- longer task horizons,
- multimodal perception,
- tool/API/MCP use,
- stateful workplace or enterprise workflows,
- safety and harmful action detection,
- deep research and source-grounded synthesis,
- or cost/reproducibility/leaderboard infrastructure.

---

## Key evidence to extract from the paper

When reading this paper, extract these concrete evidence points:

- **Android environment design.**
- **Task categories and action space.**
- **Success-rate comparisons across agents.**

Also extract, if available:

- number of tasks / instances / environments,
- task domains,
- evaluated models or scaffolds,
- main metrics,
- strongest and weakest model results,
- failure taxonomy,
- and authors' stated limitations.

---

## Limitations

- **Limitation 1:** Android UI automation differs from desktop/web automation.
- **Limitation 2:** Dynamic apps create non-determinism and reproducibility challenges.
- **Limitation 3:** Task success depends on the agent’s perception/action interface.

General limitation for your thesis:

```text
A benchmark evaluates only a slice of agent behavior.
No single benchmark proves that an agent can perform generalized web automation and data extraction.
```

Therefore, use this paper as a **P2 supporting source**, not as the sole basis for a central claim.

---

---

## Simple understanding

AI Agents That Matter is an evaluation-methodology paper arguing that agent evaluations often suffer from weak baselines, inconsistent protocols, and overclaiming.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

Provide methodological guidance for evaluating AI agents rigorously and meaningfully.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
agent evaluation methodology
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Agent results can be misleading when evaluations ignore cost, robustness, reproducibility, environment setup, ablations, and baseline quality.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

## Simple understanding

Spider2-V: How Far Are Multimodal Agents From Automating Data Science and Engineering Workflows? evaluates computer-use, desktop, office, or workplace agents. Its main value is testing whether agents can complete realistic software tasks in stateful digital workspaces.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.

## Core idea

Benchmark agents on desktop or workplace workflows involving UI interaction, application state, documents, files, or enterprise tools.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
data science / engineering workflow benchmark
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Computer-use agents need more than reasoning: they need UI grounding, state management, app-specific knowledge, and safe execution.

## Simple understanding

Windows Agent Arena: Evaluating Multi-Modal OS Agents at Scale evaluates computer-use, desktop, office, or workplace agents. Its main value is testing whether agents can complete realistic software tasks in stateful digital workspaces.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Benchmark agents on desktop or workplace workflows involving UI interaction, application state, documents, files, or enterprise tools.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
Windows OS agent benchmark
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Computer-use agents need more than reasoning: they need UI grounding, state management, app-specific knowledge, and safe execution.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.

## Metadata

- **Title:** Agent-as-a-Judge: Evaluate Agents with Agents
- **Year:** 2025
- **Verified venue/status:** ICML 2025
- **Peer-reviewed status:** Yes

- **Priority:** P2
- **Recommended citation strength:** Strong citation
- **Recommended reading depth:** Medium to high
- **BibTeX key:** `agentjudgeevaluateagents2025`

---

## Simple understanding

Agent-as-a-Judge studies whether agents themselves can be used to evaluate other agents. It responds to the difficulty of grading long, interactive trajectories.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Use agentic evaluators that inspect trajectories, interact with environments or tools, and judge task completion.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
agent-as-judge evaluation
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Agent-based judging can better match complex task evaluation than simple output-only LLM judges, but reliability and bias remain concerns.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

- more realistic environments,

- evaluated models or scaffolds,
- main metrics,
- strongest and weakest model results,
- failure taxonomy,
- and authors' stated limitations.

- **Limitation 1:** Judges can inherit model biases and hallucinations.
- **Limitation 2:** Agent-as-judge can be expensive due to extra tool use and reasoning.
- **Limitation 3:** Evaluation quality depends on judge prompts, evidence access, and task rubrics.

# S5.1 P2 Paper 20 — Survey on Evaluation of LLM-based Agents

## Metadata

- **Title:** Survey on Evaluation of LLM-based Agents
- **Year:** 2025
- **Verified venue/status:** arXiv 2503.16416
- **Peer-reviewed status:** No
- **Corrected action:** Keep as survey preprint
- **Thesis section:** S5.1 — Evaluation and Benchmarks for LLM-based Agents
- **Main category:** agent evaluation survey
- **S5.1 role:** agent evaluation methodology source
- **Priority:** P2
- **Recommended citation strength:** Support/recent-trend citation
- **Recommended reading depth:** Low to medium
- **BibTeX key:** `surveyevaluationllmbased2025`

---

## Simple understanding

Survey on Evaluation of LLM-based Agents is a survey/review of agent evaluation or computer-use agents. Its value is taxonomy, terminology, comparison of benchmarks, and open challenges.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Synthesize prior work on agent evaluation, benchmarks, capabilities, and failure modes.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
agent evaluation survey
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Agent evaluation is fragmented across domains, metrics, environments, and scaffolds, making standardized comparison difficult.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

- more realistic environments,
- longer task horizons,
- multimodal perception,
- tool/API/MCP use,
- stateful workplace or enterprise workflows,
- safety and harmful action detection,
- deep research and source-grounded synthesis,
- or cost/reproducibility/leaderboard infrastructure.

---

## Key evidence to extract from the paper

When reading this paper, extract these concrete evidence points:

- **Taxonomy tables.**
- **Benchmark comparison tables.**
- **Open challenges and future research directions.**

Also extract, if available:

- number of tasks / instances / environments,
- task domains,
- evaluated models or scaffolds,
- main metrics,
- strongest and weakest model results,
- failure taxonomy,
- and authors' stated limitations.

---

## Limitations

- **Limitation 1:** Survey papers are secondary sources and should not replace primary benchmark papers.
- **Limitation 2:** Recent survey taxonomies can become outdated quickly.
- **Limitation 3:** Broad surveys may not deeply evaluate each benchmark.

General limitation for your thesis:

```text
A benchmark evaluates only a slice of agent behavior.
No single benchmark proves that an agent can perform generalized web automation and data extraction.
```

Therefore, use this paper as a **P2 supporting source**, not as the sole basis for a central claim.

- **Year:** 2025

- **Recommended reading depth:** Medium

---

## Simple understanding

Open CaptchaWorld: A Comprehensive Web-based Platform for Testing and Benchmarking Multimodal LLM Agents evaluates web agents or web-based information tasks. Its main value is testing whether agents can search, browse, traverse, retrieve, synthesize, or act on web environments.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Benchmark LLM-based agents on web tasks requiring navigation, retrieval, reasoning, and final answer or task completion.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
captcha/web multimodal benchmark
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Web agents often perform well on short/simple tasks but fail on long-horizon navigation, dynamic pages, multilingual content, broad search, or noisy evidence.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

- more realistic environments,
- longer task horizons,
- multimodal perception,
- tool/API/MCP use,
- stateful workplace or enterprise workflows,
- safety and harmful action detection,
- deep research and source-grounded synthesis,
- or cost/reproducibility/leaderboard infrastructure.

---

## Key evidence to extract from the paper

When reading this paper, extract these concrete evidence points:

- **Task/environment design.**
- **Evaluation metrics such as success rate, key-point recall, answer accuracy, NDCG/Recall, or trajectory completion.**
- **Failure analysis for browsing or retrieval mistakes.**

Also extract, if available:

- number of tasks / instances / environments,
- task domains,
- evaluated models or scaffolds,
- main metrics,
- strongest and weakest model results,
- failure taxonomy,
- and authors' stated limitations.

---

## Limitations

- **Limitation 1:** Web environments are dynamic and hard to reproduce.
- **Limitation 2:** LLM-based or trajectory-based evaluation can be noisy.
- **Limitation 3:** Strong results may depend on specific scaffolds, browsers, websites, or task selection.

General limitation for your thesis:

```text
A benchmark evaluates only a slice of agent behavior.
No single benchmark proves that an agent can perform generalized web automation and data extraction.
```

Therefore, use this paper as a **P2 supporting source**, not as the sole basis for a central claim.

# S5.1 P2 Paper 28 — DeepResearch Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Deep Research Agents

## Metadata

- **Title:** DeepResearch Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Deep Research Agents
- **Year:** 2025/2026
- **Verified venue/status:** OpenReview ICLR 2026 record
- **Peer-reviewed status:** Unclear / likely submission
- **Corrected action:** Use cautiously
- **Thesis section:** S5.1 — Evaluation and Benchmarks for LLM-based Agents
- **Main category:** deep research agent benchmark
- **S5.1 role:** web-agent and information-access benchmark
- **Priority:** P2
- **Recommended citation strength:** Support/recent-trend citation
- **Recommended reading depth:** Medium
- **BibTeX key:** `deepresearchbenchcomprehensivedeep2025`

---

## Simple understanding

DeepResearch Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Deep Research Agents evaluates web agents or web-based information tasks. Its main value is testing whether agents can search, browse, traverse, retrieve, synthesize, or act on web environments.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Benchmark LLM-based agents on web tasks requiring navigation, retrieval, reasoning, and final answer or task completion.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
deep research agent benchmark
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Web agents often perform well on short/simple tasks but fail on long-horizon navigation, dynamic pages, multilingual content, broad search, or noisy evidence.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

- more realistic environments,
- longer task horizons,
- multimodal perception,
- tool/API/MCP use,
- stateful workplace or enterprise workflows,
- safety and harmful action detection,
- deep research and source-grounded synthesis,
- or cost/reproducibility/leaderboard infrastructure.

---

## Key evidence to extract from the paper

When reading this paper, extract these concrete evidence points:

- **Task/environment design.**
- **Evaluation metrics such as success rate, key-point recall, answer accuracy, NDCG/Recall, or trajectory completion.**
- **Failure analysis for browsing or retrieval mistakes.**

Also extract, if available:

- number of tasks / instances / environments,
- task domains,
- evaluated models or scaffolds,
- main metrics,
- strongest and weakest model results,
- failure taxonomy,
- and authors' stated limitations.

---

## Limitations

- **Limitation 1:** Web environments are dynamic and hard to reproduce.
- **Limitation 2:** LLM-based or trajectory-based evaluation can be noisy.
- **Limitation 3:** Strong results may depend on specific scaffolds, browsers, websites, or task selection.

General limitation for your thesis:

```text
A benchmark evaluates only a slice of agent behavior.
No single benchmark proves that an agent can perform generalized web automation and data extraction.
```

Therefore, use this paper as a **P2 supporting source**, not as the sole basis for a central claim.

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.

+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.

- more realistic environments,
- longer task horizons,
- multimodal perception,
- tool/API/MCP use,

- or cost/reproducibility/leaderboard infrastructure.

When reading this paper, extract these concrete evidence points:

- **Task/environment design.**
- **Evaluation metrics such as success rate, key-point recall, answer accuracy, NDCG/Recall, or trajectory completion.**

## Limitations

- **Limitation 1:** Web environments are dynamic and hard to reproduce.
- **Limitation 2:** LLM-based or trajectory-based evaluation can be noisy.

## Simple understanding

# S5.1 P2 Paper 52 — Evaluating Long-Context Reasoning in LLM-Based WebAgents

## Metadata

- **Title:** Evaluating Long-Context Reasoning in LLM-Based WebAgents
- **Year:** 2025
- **Verified venue/status:** NeurIPS 2025 LAW Workshop
- **Peer-reviewed status:** Workshop
- **Corrected action:** Use as workshop/preprint
- **Thesis section:** S5.1 — Evaluation and Benchmarks for LLM-based Agents
- **Main category:** long-context web-agent evaluation
- **S5.1 role:** web-agent and information-access benchmark
- **Priority:** P2
- **Recommended citation strength:** Medium citation
- **Recommended reading depth:** Medium
- **BibTeX key:** `longcontextreasoningllm2025`

---

## Simple understanding

This paper evaluates long-context reasoning in LLM-based WebAgents under multi-session, noisy interaction histories.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Create sequentially dependent subtasks where agents must retrieve and apply information from previous trajectories across contexts up to 150k tokens.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
long-context web-agent evaluation
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

WebAgent success drops dramatically as context length increases, often from around 40–50% in baseline settings to below 10% with long noisy contexts; agents get stuck in loops and lose objectives.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

- more realistic environments,
- longer task horizons,
- multimodal perception,

- or cost/reproducibility/leaderboard infrastructure.

---

## Key evidence to extract from the paper

When reading this paper, extract these concrete evidence points:

- **Context injection framework with 25k to 150k tokens.**
- **Sequential subtask A1/A2 design requiring memory of earlier interaction.**
- **Error analysis showing looping and objective loss.**

Also extract, if available:

- number of tasks / instances / environments,
- task domains,
- evaluated models or scaffolds,
- main metrics,
- strongest and weakest model results,
- failure taxonomy,
- and authors' stated limitations.

---

## Limitations

- **Limitation 1:** Workshop status.
- **Limitation 2:** Live-web evaluation can be noisy and hard to reproduce.
- **Limitation 3:** Implicit RAG gives only modest improvements, so the core limitation remains open.

## Simple understanding

AgentWebBench evaluates the emerging Agentic Web where a user agent must coordinate with website-specific content agents rather than directly accessing a centralized corpus.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.
```

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.  
Only after that should it be linked to S5.1 and the thesis.

---

## Core idea

Benchmark multi-agent coordination for web search, recommendation, QA, and deep research across 100 content agents and 18.4M documents.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
multi-agent coordination in agentic web benchmark
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

Decentralized coordination generally lags behind centralized retrieval, but the gap shrinks with model scale and can reverse on question answering.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.  
Most P2 papers in S5.1 extend evaluation into one of these dimensions:

- more realistic environments,

- multimodal perception,
- tool/API/MCP use,
- stateful workplace or enterprise workflows,
- safety and harmful action detection,
- deep research and source-grounded synthesis,
- or cost/reproducibility/leaderboard infrastructure.

---

## Key evidence to extract from the paper

When reading this paper, extract these concrete evidence points:

- **100 websites and 18,427,770 documents from ClueWeb22-B.**
- **Tasks: web search, web recommendation, question answering, and deep research.**
- **Comparison of ToolE, ToolP, and Multi-Agent coordination strategies.**

Also extract, if available:

- and authors' stated limitations.

---

## Limitations

- **Limitation 1:** It is a preprint in the verified report.
- **Limitation 2:** The agentic-web paradigm is emerging, so assumptions may change.
- **Limitation 3:** Multi-agent coordination introduces traffic concentration and evidence-quality risks.

General limitation for your thesis:

```text
A benchmark evaluates only a slice of agent behavior.
No single benchmark proves that an agent can perform generalized web automation and data extraction.
```

Therefore, use this paper as a **P2 supporting source**, not as the sole basis for a central claim.

---

## Simple understanding

CLAWSBENCH evaluates LLM productivity agents in realistic simulated workspaces with Gmail, Slack, Calendar, Docs, and Drive mock services.

In simple terms:

```text
Problem → Agent evaluation is difficult because agents act over time, use tools, interact with environments, and can fail in many hidden ways.
Paper → This work proposes or analyzes a benchmark, evaluation framework, or evaluation methodology.
Goal → Measure agent capability more realistically than simple text-only QA or isolated reasoning tasks.

The first goal is to understand **what the benchmark or evaluation paper measures**.

---

## Core idea

Provide high-fidelity, stateful mock productivity services with separated performance and safety scoring.

The paper mainly evaluates this capability:

```text
productivity-agent safety and capability benchmark
```

For agent evaluation, this matters because a web/GUI/computer-use agent is not judged only by its final answer. It must also be judged by:

```text
task success
+ trajectory quality
+ tool/action correctness
+ factual grounding
+ state changes
+ efficiency/cost
+ safety/reliability
+ recovery from failure
```

---

## Key finding / main claim

With full scaffolding, agents achieve 39–64% task success but still show unsafe action rates of 7–33%, revealing that capability and safety do not consistently align.

For S5.1, the important point is to identify **what new evaluation gap** this paper covers.

## Source: `S5_1_P2_paper_first_detailed_markdown_notes\S5_1_P2_GLOBAL_SYNTHESIS.md`

# S5.1 P2 — Global Synthesis

## What this S5.1 P2 batch covers

The S5.1 P2 papers are mainly about **evaluation and benchmarks for LLM-based agents**. They cover:

1. Web-agent benchmarks.
2. GUI/mobile/desktop benchmarks.
3. Office/workplace/productivity benchmarks.
4. Tool-use and MCP benchmarks.
5. Deep research and broad information-seeking benchmarks.
6. E-commerce and shopping benchmarks.
7. Safety, reward, and agent-as-judge evaluation.
8. Long-context and long-horizon evaluation.
9. Holistic leaderboard and evaluation infrastructure.

## Main thesis value

This batch is important because your thesis cannot only discuss agent architectures. It also needs to explain:

```text
How do we know whether an LLM-based web agent really works?
```

S5.1 should therefore cover:

- task success,
- partial completion,
- action accuracy,
- trajectory quality,
- factuality and citation grounding,
- state-based evaluation,
- safety and harmful action rates,
- cost and latency,
- reproducibility,
- generalization across websites/apps,
- and robustness to long contexts or dynamic environments.

## Recommended writing strategy

Use peer-reviewed benchmarks as the backbone:

```text
TravelPlanner
AndroidWorld
WorkBench
AI Agents That Matter
Spider2-V
CRAB
VisualAgentBench
Windows Agent Arena
SPA-Bench
Agent-as-a-Judge
WebOlympus
SWE-bench Goes Live
X-WebAgentBench
Agent-RewardBench
FormFactory
τ-bench
WebWalker
MCPMark
DRBench
TheMCPCompany
```

Use preprints and workshops for recent trends:

```text
SCUBA
NEWSAGENT
OSWorld-MCP
EcomBench
AGENCYBENCH
Ego2Web
AgentWebBench
CLAWSBENCH
MacroBench
GEM
```

## Key synthesis argument

A strong S5.1 paragraph can say:

```text
Evaluation of LLM-based agents has moved from static answer accuracy toward interactive, stateful, long-horizon, and safety-aware benchmarks. Early web and GUI benchmarks focused on action prediction or task completion, while recent benchmarks increasingly evaluate tool use, MCP invocation, enterprise workflows, deep research, productivity safety, long-context reasoning, and multi-agent coordination. This shift reflects the fact that generalized web automation cannot be measured by final text alone; it requires evaluation of actions, state changes, evidence grounding, cost, safety, and recovery.
```

## Final recommendation

Keep all 56 P2 papers, but do not cite all of them in the final thesis. Use them as a reserve pool for:

- examples,
- taxonomy,
- recent trends,
- evaluation gaps,
- and limitations.


---
